========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I kind of touched on this a bit with the work stuff, but it really does dictate so many of my choices now that I think about it. For instance, if we absolutely have to go somewhere, I will spend hours—and I mean literally hours—mapping out driving routes just to avoid the airport. Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. I'd literally rather lose two whole days of a weekend to highway driving than even step foot on a plane. 

And then, of course, there are those work trainings I keep bringing up. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, "Oh, we're too short-staffed that week," or "I have a family obligation." I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. 

And honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, "Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home." He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is because flying has just started to feel way, way scarier than it really should. \n\nI mean, I know it's irrational. I work as an administrator at a childcare center, so I'm used to dealing with kids being scared of things that aren't actually dangerous, and I try to use that same logic on myself because I know people fly safely every single day. Millions of people, right? But just the idea of getting on a plane... even just thinking about booking a ticket or walking down the jet bridge, it makes me picture this one specific flight. \n\nIt was my honeymoon flight, actually, and we hit this really, really bad turbulence. And I know planes are built to handle that, my husband reminds me of the statistics all the time, but my brain just goes right back to that moment—like, I can still see the overhead bins shaking and feel my stomach completely drop. So even though I know, logically, that flying is perfectly fine and safe, I just can't seem to get that honeymoon flight out of my head, and it's making the whole idea of traveling just so incredibly daunting for me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just flying that makes you feel this anxious, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings of fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it really is just flying. I mean, I’ve thought about this a lot because, you know, working at the childcare center, I see kids who are just generally anxious about everything—like loud noises, new people, dogs, even just the dark. And I always wonder if maybe I’m like that, just, you know, an anxious person overall. But when I really sit down and think about it, I’m actually totally fine with other things. \n\nLike, I drive to work every day, and traffic doesn't bother me at all, even when people are driving crazy on the highway. I don't mind heights, either. My husband and I went hiking a few months ago and stood right at the edge of this huge overlook, and I felt completely calm. I mean, I enjoyed the view! And enclosed spaces are fine too, like elevators or crowded subway cars if we go into the city. I never feel that panic, that awful dropping sensation in my stomach, or that heart-racing feeling anywhere else. \n\nIt’s literally just the airplanes. Just the thought of being up in the air, trapped in that metal tube, and hitting that turbulence again. I don't have a problem with boats, either, which some people think is weird because you can get seasick or hit rough water, but for some reason, the water doesn't scare me. It’s strictly the flying. It’s so frustrating because it’s this one very specific, isolated thing, and yet it feels like it has this huge hold over my life, especially since we really want to travel more now that we're married. But yeah, outside of airplanes, I’d say I’m actually a pretty relaxed person.\n\nDoctor: Did your fear of flying start immediately after that honeymoon flight with the bad turbulence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really did. I mean, it was basically instant. We were flying back from Hawaii, which was just this gorgeous trip, you know? We spent two weeks there, and everything was perfect, and I was so relaxed, thinking about how great married life was going to be. And then on the way back, we hit that rough patch, and it just... it changed everything for me. \n\nI remember getting off that plane and telling my husband, \"I am never doing that again.\" And he laughed, thinking I was just shaken up in the moment, which makes sense, right? A lot of people say things like that when they're scared. But I was dead serious. And it's been exactly like this ever since that day. We got married in October, so it’s been... wow, it’s been about two years and five months now since that flight. \n\nAnd in all that time, the fear hasn't faded at all. If anything, it’s just settled in. Like, at first, I thought maybe if I just waited a few months, I’d forget how awful it felt, or the memory would get a little fuzzy, like how you forget the pain of a stubbed toe after a while. But no, it’s still so vivid. Two years and five months of just dreading the idea of getting on a plane, making excuses when friends suggest group trips, and trying to convince my husband that road trips are just as fun as flying somewhere tropical. So yeah, it definitely started right then and there on that flight back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding flying or travel plans because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I've been avoiding it like the plague. I mean, it's actually become this whole big logistical nightmare in my life, to be honest. Like, just last month, there was this big early childhood education conference in Chicago that my boss really wanted me to go to. It would have been great for my career, you know, networking and learning about new administrative software, which we desperately need at the center. But they were going to fly me out, and the second she mentioned booking the flight, I just... I froze. I made up this whole excuse about how my sister needed help moving that weekend, which was totally a lie, just so I wouldn't have to get on a plane. \n\nAnd it's not just work. My husband and I, we used to talk about going to Europe, or maybe going back to Hawaii for an anniversary, but now? I am the queen of the road trip. I will happily drive twelve, fourteen hours to Florida or the Carolinas just to avoid the airport. I tell everyone I just love seeing the countryside, but really, it's just so I don't have to fly. \n\nAnd on the rare occasion where we actually do have to talk about traveling—like, if his family wants us to come out for a wedding or something, and driving just isn't an option—I can't even look at flights online by myself. I literally have to have my husband sitting right there next to me on the couch. He has to hold my hand, and I make him do all the clicking on the laptop because if I do it, I'll just close the tab and back out entirely. I just can't endure it on my own. So yeah, I've basically rearranged my entire life, and my husband's life, just to make sure I never have to step foot on an airplane again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I hear people talk about panic attacks all the time, especially nowadays, right? Like, it seems like everyone on the internet is always saying, \"Oh, I had a panic attack about this,\" or \"I'm having a panic attack about that.\" But I think they just mean they're really stressed out. \n\nFor me, when I think about flying, or like when my boss brought up that Chicago trip, I definitely get that awful, dropping feeling in my stomach, and my heart starts beating really fast, like I mentioned before. And my hands get a little sweaty, and I just feel this overwhelming sense of dread, like I just need to get out of the conversation or get away from the computer screen. But I've never, like, passed out, or felt like I couldn't breathe at all, or thought I was having a heart attack or dying, which is what I always thought a real panic attack was supposed to feel like. \n\nLike, my cousin Sarah, she had a panic attack once at a shopping mall, and she said she literally had to sit on the floor by a pretzel stand because she thought her chest was caving in and she was going to die right there. She actually had someone call an ambulance, which is so scary to even think about. I’ve never had anything like that happen to me. It’s more just this really intense, focused fear where my body goes on high alert, but I can still function, you know? I can still talk my way out of the trip or walk away from the laptop. So, no, I don't think I've ever had an actual panic attack, just really, really bad anxiety when it comes to the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your relationships with friends or family in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no, I guess. I mean, my day-to-day life is mostly fine. I'm still doing everything I need to do at the center, managing the schedules, dealing with the parents, all that stuff. And at home, my husband and I are great, we really are. He's so patient with me. But... it is starting to sort of nibble away at the edges of things, if that makes sense? \n\nLike with work, I mentioned that Chicago conference, but it's not just that one. There have been a couple of other trainings, too, things my supervisor has specifically brought up to me, saying, \"Hey, this would be a really great opportunity for you to grow into a director role eventually.\" And I just keep finding reasons not to go if it involves a flight. I mean, I'm passing up things that could really help my career, and I know I am, but I just can't bring myself to do it. I'll take all the local workshops I can find, but it's not the same.\n\nAnd then personally, it's definitely putting a damper on our plans. Before we got married, my husband and I had this whole timeline for places we wanted to see before we even thought about having kids. We had this trip to Florence that we were so excited about. We used to look at pictures of the Duomo and talk about all the pasta we were going to eat, and it was going to be this big, romantic adventure. But now? I just keep pushing it back. Every time he brings it up, I say, \"Oh, maybe next year, let's just save up a little more,\" or \"Let's do a road trip to Maine instead this summer.\" And he agrees, because he's sweet and doesn't want to push me, but I know it's disappointing for him. I know he really wants to go to Italy, and I do too, I really do! But the thought of being on a plane over the ocean for that many hours... I just can't. So yeah, it's not ruining my life completely, I'm still functioning, but it's definitely holding me back from things I actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine or activities, outside of work and travel, that your fear of flying is affecting?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really in my day-to-day routine, I guess. I mean, like I said, I'm still doing everything I need to do every day. I get up, I go to the center, I manage the schedules, I talk to the parents, I make dinner, all of that is totally fine. It's not like I'm sitting at home all day terrified to go to the grocery store or anything like that. My daily life is completely normal. \n\nBut it's just those bigger things, you know? Like I was just saying about the work stuff. I'm still managing my job just fine, but I've started passing up all those trainings and conferences that my supervisor keeps bringing up. She really thinks they would help me grow into that director role, and I know she's right, but I just can't do it if it means getting on a plane. And it's just so frustrating because I really do want to move up in my career. I've been at the center for a while now, and I feel like I'm ready for more responsibility, but this is just standing in my way. \n\nAnd then, of course, there's the personal stuff, like that trip to Florence I was telling you about. It just keeps pushing back all our personal plans. We were so excited about it, and we had all these ideas of what we were going to do and see, and now it's just... on hold indefinitely. And it's not just Florence, it's really any big trip that would require flying. We just don't even talk about it as a real possibility anymore, which is just really sad when I stop and think about it. So, outside of work and travel, my daily routine is fine, but it's definitely affecting the bigger picture of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered seeking help or therapy specifically for your fear of flying before coming here today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, no, not really. I mean, not until now, obviously. It's funny because you'd think I would have, considering how much it bothers me, but I always just thought it was something I had to live with, you know? Like, some people are afraid of spiders, some people are afraid of heights, and I'm just afraid of airplanes. I thought it was just a quirk, a personality trait or something. \n\nAnd for a long time, it was pretty easy to avoid. When I was in college, I didn't have the money to travel anyway, so it never really came up. My friends and I would just drive down to the beach for spring break, or take the train into the city for the weekend. It was all very local. And then when I started working at the center, I was just a junior staff member, so nobody was asking me to go to conferences in Chicago or anywhere else. So it was just this thing that was there, in the background, but it didn't really demand my attention. \n\nI guess I also sort of told myself that I would just grow out of it? Like, maybe one day I'd wake up and just feel ready to book a flight to Europe. But of course, that hasn't happened. And now that it's actually starting to get in the way of things—like the director role, and the trip to Florence with my husband—it's become much harder to ignore. My husband actually suggested I talk to someone about it a few months ago, after we had this whole conversation about our anniversary plans and I ended up crying because I was so frustrated with myself. He was really gentle about it, just saying, \"You know, you don't have to carry this all by yourself, maybe there's something that could help.\" But even then, it took me a while to actually make the appointment. I kept putting it off, thinking, \"Oh, I'll just try meditation,\" or \"I'll read a book about how airplanes work, maybe if I understand the aerodynamics better I won't be so scared.\" But none of that really worked. So, here I am. But yeah, before this, I never seriously looked into therapy for it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling unhappy or unfulfilled in general because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Unhappy in general? Um... I don't know if I'd say unhappy in general, because, like I was saying earlier, I really do love my day-to-day life. I love my husband, I love the center, and I'm managing my job and my home life just fine. But... unfulfilled? Yeah, I think that's actually a really good word for it. Because it really all comes back to what I was just talking about, you know? \n\nIt’s like, I sit at my desk at work, and I know I'm doing a good job with the parents and the schedules, but then I remember that I’m actively passing up those trainings and conferences. And every time I have to tell my supervisor, \"Oh, no, I don't think I can make that one,\" even though she literally just told me it would help me grow and get closer to that director role... I just feel this awful pit in my stomach. It feels like I'm putting this artificial ceiling on my own potential. I mean, how am I supposed to feel totally fulfilled in my career when I know I'm the one standing in my own way? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing at home. We have a great marriage, but then I think about how I just keep pushing back our personal plans, like that Florence trip I mentioned. It's not just about missing out on a vacation, it's about missing out on... I don't know, big life experiences? Like, we used to have these Italy travel guidebooks sitting out on our coffee table, and we'd flip through them on Sunday mornings and dream about it. But a few months ago, I actually just gathered them all up and shoved them in a drawer in the spare room because looking at them just made me feel so incredibly guilty and stuck. \n\nSo, no, my life isn't a misery or anything, I'm managing everything I need to manage on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I'd been so excited about. It's just... it's really frustrating to feel like you're shrinking your own world down, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or situations because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I kind of touched on this a bit with the work stuff, but it really does dictate so many of my choices now that I think about it. For instance, if we absolutely have to go somewhere, I will spend hours—and I mean literally hours—mapping out driving routes just to avoid the airport. Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. I'd literally rather lose two whole days of a weekend to highway driving than even step foot on a plane. \n\nAnd then, of course, there are those work trainings I keep bringing up. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, \"Oh, we're too short-staffed that week,\" or \"I have a family obligation.\" I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. \n\nAnd honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, \"Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home.\" He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. Daily activities? Um, no, not really. I mean, thankfully, right? Because if it started messing with my everyday life, I think I'd go crazy. But no, my daily routine is pretty much completely untouched by it. 

Like, I get up, I drive to the center—and I actually really like my commute, it's only about fifteen minutes and I usually listen to an audiobook. Right now I'm listening to this really great historical fiction novel, which is one of my main hobbies, actually, just reading. And obviously, you don't need to get on an airplane to read a book! So that's totally fine. And then at work, like I was saying, I'm managing the schedules and talking to the parents, and none of that triggers the fear because, well, we're firmly on the ground. 

When I get home, my husband and I really like to cook together. We've been trying out these new recipes from a cookbook his sister got us for Christmas, lots of Mediterranean stuff, which is really fun and gives us something to do in the evenings. And on the weekends, I do some gardening in the backyard, or we'll go to the local farmer's market... so yeah, my hobbies are all very much home-based or local. 

It's really only when the concept of long-distance travel comes up that I hit that wall. If I'm just living my normal Tuesday, or going about my regular weekend errands, I don't even think about airplanes. The fear is completely isolated to the actual act of having to fly somewhere. So, no, I haven't had to stop doing anything I love on a daily basis, thank goodness. I can still do my morning yoga in the living room, I can still go out to dinner with my friends, I can still organize all the childcare programs... it's just the big trips. Which, like I was saying before, is why it's so incredibly frustrating, because everything else in my daily life is going so well, you know? It's just this one very specific thing hanging over me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is because flying has just started to feel way, way scarier than it really should. \n\nI mean, I know it's irrational. I work as an administrator at a childcare center, so I'm used to dealing with kids being scared of things that aren't actually dangerous, and I try to use that same logic on myself because I know people fly safely every single day. Millions of people, right? But just the idea of getting on a plane... even just thinking about booking a ticket or walking down the jet bridge, it makes me picture this one specific flight. \n\nIt was my honeymoon flight, actually, and we hit this really, really bad turbulence. And I know planes are built to handle that, my husband reminds me of the statistics all the time, but my brain just goes right back to that moment—like, I can still see the overhead bins shaking and feel my stomach completely drop. So even though I know, logically, that flying is perfectly fine and safe, I just can't seem to get that honeymoon flight out of my head, and it's making the whole idea of traveling just so incredibly daunting for me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just flying that makes you feel this anxious, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings of fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it really is just flying. I mean, I’ve thought about this a lot because, you know, working at the childcare center, I see kids who are just generally anxious about everything—like loud noises, new people, dogs, even just the dark. And I always wonder if maybe I’m like that, just, you know, an anxious person overall. But when I really sit down and think about it, I’m actually totally fine with other things. \n\nLike, I drive to work every day, and traffic doesn't bother me at all, even when people are driving crazy on the highway. I don't mind heights, either. My husband and I went hiking a few months ago and stood right at the edge of this huge overlook, and I felt completely calm. I mean, I enjoyed the view! And enclosed spaces are fine too, like elevators or crowded subway cars if we go into the city. I never feel that panic, that awful dropping sensation in my stomach, or that heart-racing feeling anywhere else. \n\nIt’s literally just the airplanes. Just the thought of being up in the air, trapped in that metal tube, and hitting that turbulence again. I don't have a problem with boats, either, which some people think is weird because you can get seasick or hit rough water, but for some reason, the water doesn't scare me. It’s strictly the flying. It’s so frustrating because it’s this one very specific, isolated thing, and yet it feels like it has this huge hold over my life, especially since we really want to travel more now that we're married. But yeah, outside of airplanes, I’d say I’m actually a pretty relaxed person.\n\nDoctor: Did your fear of flying start immediately after that honeymoon flight with the bad turbulence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really did. I mean, it was basically instant. We were flying back from Hawaii, which was just this gorgeous trip, you know? We spent two weeks there, and everything was perfect, and I was so relaxed, thinking about how great married life was going to be. And then on the way back, we hit that rough patch, and it just... it changed everything for me. \n\nI remember getting off that plane and telling my husband, \"I am never doing that again.\" And he laughed, thinking I was just shaken up in the moment, which makes sense, right? A lot of people say things like that when they're scared. But I was dead serious. And it's been exactly like this ever since that day. We got married in October, so it’s been... wow, it’s been about two years and five months now since that flight. \n\nAnd in all that time, the fear hasn't faded at all. If anything, it’s just settled in. Like, at first, I thought maybe if I just waited a few months, I’d forget how awful it felt, or the memory would get a little fuzzy, like how you forget the pain of a stubbed toe after a while. But no, it’s still so vivid. Two years and five months of just dreading the idea of getting on a plane, making excuses when friends suggest group trips, and trying to convince my husband that road trips are just as fun as flying somewhere tropical. So yeah, it definitely started right then and there on that flight back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding flying or travel plans because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I've been avoiding it like the plague. I mean, it's actually become this whole big logistical nightmare in my life, to be honest. Like, just last month, there was this big early childhood education conference in Chicago that my boss really wanted me to go to. It would have been great for my career, you know, networking and learning about new administrative software, which we desperately need at the center. But they were going to fly me out, and the second she mentioned booking the flight, I just... I froze. I made up this whole excuse about how my sister needed help moving that weekend, which was totally a lie, just so I wouldn't have to get on a plane. \n\nAnd it's not just work. My husband and I, we used to talk about going to Europe, or maybe going back to Hawaii for an anniversary, but now? I am the queen of the road trip. I will happily drive twelve, fourteen hours to Florida or the Carolinas just to avoid the airport. I tell everyone I just love seeing the countryside, but really, it's just so I don't have to fly. \n\nAnd on the rare occasion where we actually do have to talk about traveling—like, if his family wants us to come out for a wedding or something, and driving just isn't an option—I can't even look at flights online by myself. I literally have to have my husband sitting right there next to me on the couch. He has to hold my hand, and I make him do all the clicking on the laptop because if I do it, I'll just close the tab and back out entirely. I just can't endure it on my own. So yeah, I've basically rearranged my entire life, and my husband's life, just to make sure I never have to step foot on an airplane again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I hear people talk about panic attacks all the time, especially nowadays, right? Like, it seems like everyone on the internet is always saying, \"Oh, I had a panic attack about this,\" or \"I'm having a panic attack about that.\" But I think they just mean they're really stressed out. \n\nFor me, when I think about flying, or like when my boss brought up that Chicago trip, I definitely get that awful, dropping feeling in my stomach, and my heart starts beating really fast, like I mentioned before. And my hands get a little sweaty, and I just feel this overwhelming sense of dread, like I just need to get out of the conversation or get away from the computer screen. But I've never, like, passed out, or felt like I couldn't breathe at all, or thought I was having a heart attack or dying, which is what I always thought a real panic attack was supposed to feel like. \n\nLike, my cousin Sarah, she had a panic attack once at a shopping mall, and she said she literally had to sit on the floor by a pretzel stand because she thought her chest was caving in and she was going to die right there. She actually had someone call an ambulance, which is so scary to even think about. I’ve never had anything like that happen to me. It’s more just this really intense, focused fear where my body goes on high alert, but I can still function, you know? I can still talk my way out of the trip or walk away from the laptop. So, no, I don't think I've ever had an actual panic attack, just really, really bad anxiety when it comes to the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your relationships with friends or family in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no, I guess. I mean, my day-to-day life is mostly fine. I'm still doing everything I need to do at the center, managing the schedules, dealing with the parents, all that stuff. And at home, my husband and I are great, we really are. He's so patient with me. But... it is starting to sort of nibble away at the edges of things, if that makes sense? \n\nLike with work, I mentioned that Chicago conference, but it's not just that one. There have been a couple of other trainings, too, things my supervisor has specifically brought up to me, saying, \"Hey, this would be a really great opportunity for you to grow into a director role eventually.\" And I just keep finding reasons not to go if it involves a flight. I mean, I'm passing up things that could really help my career, and I know I am, but I just can't bring myself to do it. I'll take all the local workshops I can find, but it's not the same.\n\nAnd then personally, it's definitely putting a damper on our plans. Before we got married, my husband and I had this whole timeline for places we wanted to see before we even thought about having kids. We had this trip to Florence that we were so excited about. We used to look at pictures of the Duomo and talk about all the pasta we were going to eat, and it was going to be this big, romantic adventure. But now? I just keep pushing it back. Every time he brings it up, I say, \"Oh, maybe next year, let's just save up a little more,\" or \"Let's do a road trip to Maine instead this summer.\" And he agrees, because he's sweet and doesn't want to push me, but I know it's disappointing for him. I know he really wants to go to Italy, and I do too, I really do! But the thought of being on a plane over the ocean for that many hours... I just can't. So yeah, it's not ruining my life completely, I'm still functioning, but it's definitely holding me back from things I actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine or activities, outside of work and travel, that your fear of flying is affecting?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really in my day-to-day routine, I guess. I mean, like I said, I'm still doing everything I need to do every day. I get up, I go to the center, I manage the schedules, I talk to the parents, I make dinner, all of that is totally fine. It's not like I'm sitting at home all day terrified to go to the grocery store or anything like that. My daily life is completely normal. \n\nBut it's just those bigger things, you know? Like I was just saying about the work stuff. I'm still managing my job just fine, but I've started passing up all those trainings and conferences that my supervisor keeps bringing up. She really thinks they would help me grow into that director role, and I know she's right, but I just can't do it if it means getting on a plane. And it's just so frustrating because I really do want to move up in my career. I've been at the center for a while now, and I feel like I'm ready for more responsibility, but this is just standing in my way. \n\nAnd then, of course, there's the personal stuff, like that trip to Florence I was telling you about. It just keeps pushing back all our personal plans. We were so excited about it, and we had all these ideas of what we were going to do and see, and now it's just... on hold indefinitely. And it's not just Florence, it's really any big trip that would require flying. We just don't even talk about it as a real possibility anymore, which is just really sad when I stop and think about it. So, outside of work and travel, my daily routine is fine, but it's definitely affecting the bigger picture of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered seeking help or therapy specifically for your fear of flying before coming here today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, no, not really. I mean, not until now, obviously. It's funny because you'd think I would have, considering how much it bothers me, but I always just thought it was something I had to live with, you know? Like, some people are afraid of spiders, some people are afraid of heights, and I'm just afraid of airplanes. I thought it was just a quirk, a personality trait or something. \n\nAnd for a long time, it was pretty easy to avoid. When I was in college, I didn't have the money to travel anyway, so it never really came up. My friends and I would just drive down to the beach for spring break, or take the train into the city for the weekend. It was all very local. And then when I started working at the center, I was just a junior staff member, so nobody was asking me to go to conferences in Chicago or anywhere else. So it was just this thing that was there, in the background, but it didn't really demand my attention. \n\nI guess I also sort of told myself that I would just grow out of it? Like, maybe one day I'd wake up and just feel ready to book a flight to Europe. But of course, that hasn't happened. And now that it's actually starting to get in the way of things—like the director role, and the trip to Florence with my husband—it's become much harder to ignore. My husband actually suggested I talk to someone about it a few months ago, after we had this whole conversation about our anniversary plans and I ended up crying because I was so frustrated with myself. He was really gentle about it, just saying, \"You know, you don't have to carry this all by yourself, maybe there's something that could help.\" But even then, it took me a while to actually make the appointment. I kept putting it off, thinking, \"Oh, I'll just try meditation,\" or \"I'll read a book about how airplanes work, maybe if I understand the aerodynamics better I won't be so scared.\" But none of that really worked. So, here I am. But yeah, before this, I never seriously looked into therapy for it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling unhappy or unfulfilled in general because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Unhappy in general? Um... I don't know if I'd say unhappy in general, because, like I was saying earlier, I really do love my day-to-day life. I love my husband, I love the center, and I'm managing my job and my home life just fine. But... unfulfilled? Yeah, I think that's actually a really good word for it. Because it really all comes back to what I was just talking about, you know? \n\nIt’s like, I sit at my desk at work, and I know I'm doing a good job with the parents and the schedules, but then I remember that I’m actively passing up those trainings and conferences. And every time I have to tell my supervisor, \"Oh, no, I don't think I can make that one,\" even though she literally just told me it would help me grow and get closer to that director role... I just feel this awful pit in my stomach. It feels like I'm putting this artificial ceiling on my own potential. I mean, how am I supposed to feel totally fulfilled in my career when I know I'm the one standing in my own way? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing at home. We have a great marriage, but then I think about how I just keep pushing back our personal plans, like that Florence trip I mentioned. It's not just about missing out on a vacation, it's about missing out on... I don't know, big life experiences? Like, we used to have these Italy travel guidebooks sitting out on our coffee table, and we'd flip through them on Sunday mornings and dream about it. But a few months ago, I actually just gathered them all up and shoved them in a drawer in the spare room because looking at them just made me feel so incredibly guilty and stuck. \n\nSo, no, my life isn't a misery or anything, I'm managing everything I need to manage on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I'd been so excited about. It's just... it's really frustrating to feel like you're shrinking your own world down, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or situations because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I kind of touched on this a bit with the work stuff, but it really does dictate so many of my choices now that I think about it. For instance, if we absolutely have to go somewhere, I will spend hours—and I mean literally hours—mapping out driving routes just to avoid the airport. Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. I'd literally rather lose two whole days of a weekend to highway driving than even step foot on a plane. \n\nAnd then, of course, there are those work trainings I keep bringing up. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, \"Oh, we're too short-staffed that week,\" or \"I have a family obligation.\" I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. \n\nAnd honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, \"Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home.\" He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Daily activities? Um, no, not really. I mean, thankfully, right? Because if it started messing with my everyday life, I think I'd go crazy. But no, my daily routine is pretty much completely untouched by it. \n\nLike, I get up, I drive to the center—and I actually really like my commute, it's only about fifteen minutes and I usually listen to an audiobook. Right now I'm listening to this really great historical fiction novel, which is one of my main hobbies, actually, just reading. And obviously, you don't need to get on an airplane to read a book! So that's totally fine. And then at work, like I was saying, I'm managing the schedules and talking to the parents, and none of that triggers the fear because, well, we're firmly on the ground. \n\nWhen I get home, my husband and I really like to cook together. We've been trying out these new recipes from a cookbook his sister got us for Christmas, lots of Mediterranean stuff, which is really fun and gives us something to do in the evenings. And on the weekends, I do some gardening in the backyard, or we'll go to the local farmer's market... so yeah, my hobbies are all very much home-based or local. \n\nIt's really only when the concept of long-distance travel comes up that I hit that wall. If I'm just living my normal Tuesday, or going about my regular weekend errands, I don't even think about airplanes. The fear is completely isolated to the actual act of having to fly somewhere. So, no, I haven't had to stop doing anything I love on a daily basis, thank goodness. I can still do my morning yoga in the living room, I can still go out to dinner with my friends, I can still organize all the childcare programs... it's just the big trips. Which, like I was saying before, is why it's so incredibly frustrating, because everything else in my daily life is going so well, you know? It's just this one very specific thing hanging over me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, \"Oh, we're too short-staffed that week,\" or \"I have a family obligation.\" I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. \n\nAnd honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, \"Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home.\" He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Daily activities? But no, my daily routine is pretty much completely untouched by it. \n\nLike, I get up, I drive to the center—and I actually really like my commute, it's only about fifteen minutes and I usually listen to an audiobook. Right now I'm listening to this really great historical fiction novel, which is one of my main hobbies, actually, just reading. And obviously, you don't need to get on an airplane to read a book! So that's totally fine. And then at work, like I was saying, I'm managing the schedules and talking to the parents, and none of that triggers the fear because, well, we're firmly on the ground. \n\nWhen I get home, my husband and I really like to cook together. If I'm just living my normal Tuesday, or going about my regular weekend errands, I don't even think about airplanes. The fear is completely isolated to the actual act of having to fly somewhere. Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, I wouldn't say I'm unhappy in general, because I really do love my life, you know? I love my husband, I love my job at the center. So yeah, I'm managing everything I need to on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I really want to do, and that's just incredibly frustrating. ========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is because flying has just started to feel way, way scarier than it really should. \n\nI mean, I know it's irrational. I work as an administrator at a childcare center, so I'm used to dealing with kids being scared of things that aren't actually dangerous, and I try to use that same logic on myself because I know people fly safely every single day. Millions of people, right? But just the idea of getting on a plane... even just thinking about booking a ticket or walking down the jet bridge, it makes me picture this one specific flight. \n\nIt was my honeymoon flight, actually, and we hit this really, really bad turbulence. And I know planes are built to handle that, my husband reminds me of the statistics all the time, but my brain just goes right back to that moment—like, I can still see the overhead bins shaking and feel my stomach completely drop. So even though I know, logically, that flying is perfectly fine and safe, I just can't seem to get that honeymoon flight out of my head, and it's making the whole idea of traveling just so incredibly daunting for me.\n\nDoctor: Is it just flying that makes you feel this anxious, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings of fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it really is just flying. I mean, I’ve thought about this a lot because, you know, working at the childcare center, I see kids who are just generally anxious about everything—like loud noises, new people, dogs, even just the dark. And I always wonder if maybe I’m like that, just, you know, an anxious person overall. But when I really sit down and think about it, I’m actually totally fine with other things. \n\nLike, I drive to work every day, and traffic doesn't bother me at all, even when people are driving crazy on the highway. I don't mind heights, either. My husband and I went hiking a few months ago and stood right at the edge of this huge overlook, and I felt completely calm. I mean, I enjoyed the view! And enclosed spaces are fine too, like elevators or crowded subway cars if we go into the city. I never feel that panic, that awful dropping sensation in my stomach, or that heart-racing feeling anywhere else. \n\nIt’s literally just the airplanes. Just the thought of being up in the air, trapped in that metal tube, and hitting that turbulence again. I don't have a problem with boats, either, which some people think is weird because you can get seasick or hit rough water, but for some reason, the water doesn't scare me. It’s strictly the flying. It’s so frustrating because it’s this one very specific, isolated thing, and yet it feels like it has this huge hold over my life, especially since we really want to travel more now that we're married. But yeah, outside of airplanes, I’d say I’m actually a pretty relaxed person.\n\nDoctor: Did your fear of flying start immediately after that honeymoon flight with the bad turbulence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really did. I mean, it was basically instant. We were flying back from Hawaii, which was just this gorgeous trip, you know? We spent two weeks there, and everything was perfect, and I was so relaxed, thinking about how great married life was going to be. And then on the way back, we hit that rough patch, and it just... it changed everything for me. \n\nI remember getting off that plane and telling my husband, \"I am never doing that again.\" And he laughed, thinking I was just shaken up in the moment, which makes sense, right? A lot of people say things like that when they're scared. But I was dead serious. And it's been exactly like this ever since that day. We got married in October, so it’s been... wow, it’s been about two years and five months now since that flight. \n\nAnd in all that time, the fear hasn't faded at all. If anything, it’s just settled in. Like, at first, I thought maybe if I just waited a few months, I’d forget how awful it felt, or the memory would get a little fuzzy, like how you forget the pain of a stubbed toe after a while. But no, it’s still so vivid. Two years and five months of just dreading the idea of getting on a plane, making excuses when friends suggest group trips, and trying to convince my husband that road trips are just as fun as flying somewhere tropical. So yeah, it definitely started right then and there on that flight back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding flying or travel plans because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I've been avoiding it like the plague. I mean, it's actually become this whole big logistical nightmare in my life, to be honest. Like, just last month, there was this big early childhood education conference in Chicago that my boss really wanted me to go to. It would have been great for my career, you know, networking and learning about new administrative software, which we desperately need at the center. But they were going to fly me out, and the second she mentioned booking the flight, I just... I froze. I made up this whole excuse about how my sister needed help moving that weekend, which was totally a lie, just so I wouldn't have to get on a plane. \n\nAnd it's not just work. My husband and I, we used to talk about going to Europe, or maybe going back to Hawaii for an anniversary, but now? I am the queen of the road trip. I will happily drive twelve, fourteen hours to Florida or the Carolinas just to avoid the airport. I tell everyone I just love seeing the countryside, but really, it's just so I don't have to fly. \n\nAnd on the rare occasion where we actually do have to talk about traveling—like, if his family wants us to come out for a wedding or something, and driving just isn't an option—I can't even look at flights online by myself. I literally have to have my husband sitting right there next to me on the couch. He has to hold my hand, and I make him do all the clicking on the laptop because if I do it, I'll just close the tab and back out entirely. I just can't endure it on my own. So yeah, I've basically rearranged my entire life, and my husband's life, just to make sure I never have to step foot on an airplane again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I don't think so. I mean, I hear people talk about panic attacks all the time, especially nowadays, right? Like, it seems like everyone on the internet is always saying, \"Oh, I had a panic attack about this,\" or \"I'm having a panic attack about that.\" But I think they just mean they're really stressed out. \n\nFor me, when I think about flying, or like when my boss brought up that Chicago trip, I definitely get that awful, dropping feeling in my stomach, and my heart starts beating really fast, like I mentioned before. And my hands get a little sweaty, and I just feel this overwhelming sense of dread, like I just need to get out of the conversation or get away from the computer screen. But I've never, like, passed out, or felt like I couldn't breathe at all, or thought I was having a heart attack or dying, which is what I always thought a real panic attack was supposed to feel like. \n\nLike, my cousin Sarah, she had a panic attack once at a shopping mall, and she said she literally had to sit on the floor by a pretzel stand because she thought her chest was caving in and she was going to die right there. She actually had someone call an ambulance, which is so scary to even think about. I’ve never had anything like that happen to me. It’s more just this really intense, focused fear where my body goes on high alert, but I can still function, you know? I can still talk my way out of the trip or walk away from the laptop. So, no, I don't think I've ever had an actual panic attack, just really, really bad anxiety when it comes to the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your relationships with friends or family in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes and no, I guess. I mean, my day-to-day life is mostly fine. I'm still doing everything I need to do at the center, managing the schedules, dealing with the parents, all that stuff. And at home, my husband and I are great, we really are. He's so patient with me. But... it is starting to sort of nibble away at the edges of things, if that makes sense? \n\nLike with work, I mentioned that Chicago conference, but it's not just that one. There have been a couple of other trainings, too, things my supervisor has specifically brought up to me, saying, \"Hey, this would be a really great opportunity for you to grow into a director role eventually.\" And I just keep finding reasons not to go if it involves a flight. I mean, I'm passing up things that could really help my career, and I know I am, but I just can't bring myself to do it. I'll take all the local workshops I can find, but it's not the same.\n\nAnd then personally, it's definitely putting a damper on our plans. Before we got married, my husband and I had this whole timeline for places we wanted to see before we even thought about having kids. We had this trip to Florence that we were so excited about. We used to look at pictures of the Duomo and talk about all the pasta we were going to eat, and it was going to be this big, romantic adventure. But now? I just keep pushing it back. Every time he brings it up, I say, \"Oh, maybe next year, let's just save up a little more,\" or \"Let's do a road trip to Maine instead this summer.\" And he agrees, because he's sweet and doesn't want to push me, but I know it's disappointing for him. I know he really wants to go to Italy, and I do too, I really do! But the thought of being on a plane over the ocean for that many hours... I just can't. So yeah, it's not ruining my life completely, I'm still functioning, but it's definitely holding me back from things I actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine or activities, outside of work and travel, that your fear of flying is affecting?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really in my day-to-day routine, I guess. I mean, like I said, I'm still doing everything I need to do every day. I get up, I go to the center, I manage the schedules, I talk to the parents, I make dinner, all of that is totally fine. It's not like I'm sitting at home all day terrified to go to the grocery store or anything like that. My daily life is completely normal. \n\nBut it's just those bigger things, you know? Like I was just saying about the work stuff. I'm still managing my job just fine, but I've started passing up all those trainings and conferences that my supervisor keeps bringing up. She really thinks they would help me grow into that director role, and I know she's right, but I just can't do it if it means getting on a plane. And it's just so frustrating because I really do want to move up in my career. I've been at the center for a while now, and I feel like I'm ready for more responsibility, but this is just standing in my way. \n\nAnd then, of course, there's the personal stuff, like that trip to Florence I was telling you about. It just keeps pushing back all our personal plans. We were so excited about it, and we had all these ideas of what we were going to do and see, and now it's just... on hold indefinitely. And it's not just Florence, it's really any big trip that would require flying. We just don't even talk about it as a real possibility anymore, which is just really sad when I stop and think about it. So, outside of work and travel, my daily routine is fine, but it's definitely affecting the bigger picture of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered seeking help or therapy specifically for your fear of flying before coming here today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, no, not really. I mean, not until now, obviously. It's funny because you'd think I would have, considering how much it bothers me, but I always just thought it was something I had to live with, you know? Like, some people are afraid of spiders, some people are afraid of heights, and I'm just afraid of airplanes. I thought it was just a quirk, a personality trait or something. \n\nAnd for a long time, it was pretty easy to avoid. When I was in college, I didn't have the money to travel anyway, so it never really came up. My friends and I would just drive down to the beach for spring break, or take the train into the city for the weekend. It was all very local. And then when I started working at the center, I was just a junior staff member, so nobody was asking me to go to conferences in Chicago or anywhere else. So it was just this thing that was there, in the background, but it didn't really demand my attention. \n\nI guess I also sort of told myself that I would just grow out of it? Like, maybe one day I'd wake up and just feel ready to book a flight to Europe. But of course, that hasn't happened. And now that it's actually starting to get in the way of things—like the director role, and the trip to Florence with my husband—it's become much harder to ignore. My husband actually suggested I talk to someone about it a few months ago, after we had this whole conversation about our anniversary plans and I ended up crying because I was so frustrated with myself. He was really gentle about it, just saying, \"You know, you don't have to carry this all by yourself, maybe there's something that could help.\" But even then, it took me a while to actually make the appointment. I kept putting it off, thinking, \"Oh, I'll just try meditation,\" or \"I'll read a book about how airplanes work, maybe if I understand the aerodynamics better I won't be so scared.\" But none of that really worked. So, here I am. But yeah, before this, I never seriously looked into therapy for it.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling unhappy or unfulfilled in general because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Unhappy in general? Um... I don't know if I'd say unhappy in general, because, like I was saying earlier, I really do love my day-to-day life. I love my husband, I love the center, and I'm managing my job and my home life just fine. But... unfulfilled? Yeah, I think that's actually a really good word for it. Because it really all comes back to what I was just talking about, you know? \n\nIt’s like, I sit at my desk at work, and I know I'm doing a good job with the parents and the schedules, but then I remember that I’m actively passing up those trainings and conferences. And every time I have to tell my supervisor, \"Oh, no, I don't think I can make that one,\" even though she literally just told me it would help me grow and get closer to that director role... I just feel this awful pit in my stomach. It feels like I'm putting this artificial ceiling on my own potential. I mean, how am I supposed to feel totally fulfilled in my career when I know I'm the one standing in my own way? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing at home. We have a great marriage, but then I think about how I just keep pushing back our personal plans, like that Florence trip I mentioned. It's not just about missing out on a vacation, it's about missing out on... I don't know, big life experiences? Like, we used to have these Italy travel guidebooks sitting out on our coffee table, and we'd flip through them on Sunday mornings and dream about it. But a few months ago, I actually just gathered them all up and shoved them in a drawer in the spare room because looking at them just made me feel so incredibly guilty and stuck. \n\nSo, no, my life isn't a misery or anything, I'm managing everything I need to manage on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I'd been so excited about. It's just... it's really frustrating to feel like you're shrinking your own world down, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or situations because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I kind of touched on this a bit with the work stuff, but it really does dictate so many of my choices now that I think about it. For instance, if we absolutely have to go somewhere, I will spend hours—and I mean literally hours—mapping out driving routes just to avoid the airport. Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. I'd literally rather lose two whole days of a weekend to highway driving than even step foot on a plane. \n\nAnd then, of course, there are those work trainings I keep bringing up. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, \"Oh, we're too short-staffed that week,\" or \"I have a family obligation.\" I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. \n\nAnd honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, \"Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home.\" He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Daily activities? Um, no, not really. I mean, thankfully, right? Because if it started messing with my everyday life, I think I'd go crazy. But no, my daily routine is pretty much completely untouched by it. \n\nLike, I get up, I drive to the center—and I actually really like my commute, it's only about fifteen minutes and I usually listen to an audiobook. Right now I'm listening to this really great historical fiction novel, which is one of my main hobbies, actually, just reading. And obviously, you don't need to get on an airplane to read a book! So that's totally fine. And then at work, like I was saying, I'm managing the schedules and talking to the parents, and none of that triggers the fear because, well, we're firmly on the ground. \n\nWhen I get home, my husband and I really like to cook together. We've been trying out these new recipes from a cookbook his sister got us for Christmas, lots of Mediterranean stuff, which is really fun and gives us something to do in the evenings. And on the weekends, I do some gardening in the backyard, or we'll go to the local farmer's market... so yeah, my hobbies are all very much home-based or local. \n\nIt's really only when the concept of long-distance travel comes up that I hit that wall. If I'm just living my normal Tuesday, or going about my regular weekend errands, I don't even think about airplanes. The fear is completely isolated to the actual act of having to fly somewhere. So, no, I haven't had to stop doing anything I love on a daily basis, thank goodness. I can still do my morning yoga in the living room, I can still go out to dinner with my friends, I can still organize all the childcare programs... it's just the big trips. Which, like I was saying before, is why it's so incredibly frustrating, because everything else in my daily life is going so well, you know? It's just this one very specific thing hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is affecting your overall happiness or sense of fulfillment in life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, I wouldn't say I'm unhappy in general, because I really do love my life, you know? I mean, how am I supposed to feel totally fulfilled in my career when I know I'm the one standing in my own way? \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing at home. We have a great marriage, but then I think about how I just keep pushing back our personal plans, like that Florence trip I mentioned. It's not just about missing out on a vacation, it's about missing out on... I don't know, big life experiences? Like, we used to have these Italy travel guidebooks sitting out on our coffee table, and we'd flip through them on Sunday mornings and dream about it. But a few months ago, I actually just gathered them all up and shoved them in a drawer in the spare room because looking at them just made me feel so incredibly guilty and stuck. \n\nSo, no, my life isn't a misery or anything, I'm managing everything I need to manage on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I'd been so excited about. It's just... it's really frustrating to feel like you're shrinking your own world down, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other activities or situations because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I kind of touched on this a bit with the work stuff, but it really does dictate so many of my choices now that I think about it. For instance, if we absolutely have to go somewhere, I will spend hours—and I mean literally hours—mapping out driving routes just to avoid the airport. Like, last year we had to go to my cousin's wedding a few states over. Instead of taking what would have been, I don't know, a quick two-hour flight, I insisted we drive. It took fourteen hours each way! Fourteen hours in the car! I packed all these snacks, queued up a million podcasts, and I just tried to make it seem like this fun, spontaneous road trip, but really it was just because I couldn't bear the alternative. I'd literally rather lose two whole days of a weekend to highway driving than even step foot on a plane. \n\nAnd then, of course, there are those work trainings I keep bringing up. I just flat out turn them down. If it's not within a reasonable driving distance from the center, I just make up an excuse. I'll say, \"Oh, we're too short-staffed that week,\" or \"I have a family obligation.\" I really hate lying to my supervisor, especially since she's so supportive of my career, but I just can't bring myself to do it. \n\nAnd honestly, it’s gotten to the point where even just the planning part of a trip is too much for me to handle alone. If we absolutely have to talk about a trip—like if my husband brings up visiting his parents for the holidays or something—I can't even have the conversation by myself. I get so overwhelmed just looking at the dates or the logistics. If we have to discuss it, I literally need him sitting right next to me on the couch. Like, holding my hand, talking me through it step by step, because if he isn't right there grounding me, I will just completely back out. I'll just shut down and say, \"Nope, we're not going, we'll stay home.\" He really has to be there to help me get through just the conversation about it, otherwise I'd avoid the topic entirely and we'd never go anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Daily activities? Um, no, not really. I mean, thankfully, right? Because if it started messing with my everyday life, I think I'd go crazy. But no, my daily routine is pretty much completely untouched by it. \n\nLike, I get up, I drive to the center—and I actually really like my commute, it's only about fifteen minutes and I usually listen to an audiobook. Right now I'm listening to this really great historical fiction novel, which is one of my main hobbies, actually, just reading. And obviously, you don't need to get on an airplane to read a book! So that's totally fine. And then at work, like I was saying, I'm managing the schedules and talking to the parents, and none of that triggers the fear because, well, we're firmly on the ground. \n\nWhen I get home, my husband and I really like to cook together. We've been trying out these new recipes from a cookbook his sister got us for Christmas, lots of Mediterranean stuff, which is really fun and gives us something to do in the evenings. And on the weekends, I do some gardening in the backyard, or we'll go to the local farmer's market... so yeah, my hobbies are all very much home-based or local. \n\nIt's really only when the concept of long-distance travel comes up that I hit that wall. If I'm just living my normal Tuesday, or going about my regular weekend errands, I don't even think about airplanes. The fear is completely isolated to the actual act of having to fly somewhere. So, no, I haven't had to stop doing anything I love on a daily basis, thank goodness. I can still do my morning yoga in the living room, I can still go out to dinner with my friends, I can still organize all the childcare programs... it's just the big trips. Which, like I was saying before, is why it's so incredibly frustrating, because everything else in my daily life is going so well, you know? It's just this one very specific thing hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is affecting your overall happiness or sense of fulfillment in life?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying just a minute ago, I wouldn't say I'm unhappy in general, because I really do love my life, you know? I love my husband, I love my job at the center. I'm managing my day-to-day stuff just fine, like getting to work, taking care of the house, all of that is totally normal. But... unfulfilled? Yeah, I think that's the exact word for it. It really is. Because it's like I have this artificial ceiling I've put on myself. \n\nLike with work, I sit there and I know I'm doing a great job with the parents and the scheduling, but then I actively pass up these trainings and conferences. My supervisor is so supportive, she literally just told me going to these would help me grow and get closer to that director role I've been wanting, and I just... I make up an excuse. I tell her I can't go. And it just makes me feel this awful pit in my stomach, knowing I'm the one standing in my own way. So yeah, I'm managing everything I need to on a daily basis, but it definitely feels like there's this big, invisible wall keeping me from the things I really want to do, and that's just incredibly frustrating. Well, I guess I kind of just touched on this a second ago, right? With my husband and my boss. Because, honestly, I’m still managing my job and my home life totally fine on a day-to-day basis. We aren't having screaming matches, and I'm not getting written up at work or anything like that. But... yeah, underneath it all, there's definitely a strain starting to build up. 

Like with my husband and that Florence trip I was just telling you about. I just keep pushing back our personal plans, and even though he is incredibly sweet and patient about it, I can see the disappointment. It's in the little things, you know? Like, he had actually downloaded one of those language apps on his phone to learn some basic Italian phrases—just silly things like how to order coffee or ask for directions—and he’d practice them out loud while we were making dinner. He was just so excited. And now... he doesn't do that anymore. He hasn't opened the app in months. It just breaks my heart. I feel like I'm depriving him of the life and the adventures he thought we were going to have together, and I worry so much that eventually he's going to resent me for it. I mean, how many times can you tell your partner "maybe next year" before they just get totally fed up with you? 

And then it’s a similar kind of strain with my supervisor at the center. Like I said, my daily job is getting done perfectly, but because I’ve started passing up all those trainings and conferences she keeps sending my way... I just don't know what she thinks of me anymore. She genuinely thinks these events would help me grow and move up to a director position. She's really gone out of her way to mentor me, which is so rare to find in a boss, you know? She really advocates for me. And here I am, just constantly turning her down with these flimsy excuses. I hate it because I can't tell her the real reason, so I probably just look like I lack ambition, or like I don't appreciate her help. I’m so scared she’s just going to stop trying with me, stop believing in my potential entirely. Because why would she keep sticking her neck out for someone who won't even go to a weekend leadership seminar? It just makes me feel awful, knowing that this fear is affecting the exact people who are just trying to support me the most.
